I try to think about what I used to do for fun before all this started, before the whole clinic schedule got flipped upside down and this heavy feeling moved in, and I just draw a complete blank. I mean, I know I must have done things, right? Just normal stuff a twenty-year-old does when they aren't working. But now, when I finally clock out and get home, and my feet are just aching from running around the exam rooms all day, I just... I don't do anything. \n\nI don't even know what I *would* do. Sometimes I'll sit there on the edge of my bed and think, 'Okay, I should do something I like,' but then nothing sounds good. Nothing feels good. It's just... flat. Everything is just completely flat. It’s like I’m just trying to pass the time until it’s an acceptable hour to go to sleep, just so I don't have to feel so drained for a few hours. So, I don't really know. I haven't really noticed myself enjoying... well, anything, really. It’s just all sort of blended together into this one long, gray blur where nothing really stands out as fun or pleasurable at all. I just don't have the energy to even figure out what I might like anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had thoughts of hurting yourself or wished you were dead?\n\nPatient: No, no, I... I haven't really had thoughts like that. I mean, I know I talk a lot about how heavy everything feels, and how exhausting it is to just get through a shift at the clinic, but it's not like I want to... to hurt myself or anything. It's more just this constant, draining fatigue, you know? Like, I just want a break from feeling so low all the time. But I don't sit around wishing I was dead. I just wish things felt different. I wish I could just wake up one morning and not feel that immediate weight on my chest before I even swing my legs out of bed. \n\nSometimes, when I'm rooming my fifth patient of the morning and they're complaining about something minor, I just catch myself staring at the computer screen, charting their vitals, and I just feel so disconnected, but it never crosses over into wanting to end things. It's just... I want the grayness to stop. I want to feel like a normal twenty-year-old who can actually figure out what to do on a day off instead of just staring at the wall until it's time to sleep again. So, no, I don't have those kinds of thoughts. It's just a really deep, persistent kind of tired that I can't seem to shake, no matter what I try.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite over the past two years?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, it's funny you ask that, because... I don't really know if I'd call it a change, exactly? I mean, I guess I haven't really been paying that much attention to it, with everything else going on. When the schedule was so crazy at the clinic, I was basically just grabbing whatever I could whenever I had a second. Like, I'd eat half a protein bar while I was running between room three and room five, or I'd just chug some coffee and hope it held me over until I got home at like 8:00 PM. \n\nAnd then, even when things settled down, it just became this routine of... eating because I know I'm supposed to, I guess? I don't really think about food that much anymore. It's not like I'm starving myself or eating everything in sight. It's just... another thing I have to do to get through the day. Sometimes I'll get home and realize I haven't really eaten a real meal, so I'll just microwave something quick, but it's not because I'm craving anything. I don't really get excited about food like I used to. Like, I used to love going out and getting a burger or something on my day off, but now it just seems like too much effort to even decide what I want, let alone go get it. So I just eat whatever's easiest, just to keep going. But I wouldn't say my appetite has totally tanked or spiked or anything like that. It's just sort of... there. Like everything else, I guess. Just another part of the gray blur.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep over the past two years?\n\nPatient: Sleep? Um... honestly, I don't really know if I'd say it's changed, exactly. It’s hard to tell anymore. I mean, like I said, when I get home from the clinic, I'm just so unbelievably exhausted. My whole body just aches, and my brain feels like it's full of cotton, so you'd think I'd just pass out the second my head hits the pillow, right? But it's not really like that. \n\nI'll do my whole routine, you know, take a shower to try and wash the clinic smell off me, maybe microwave whatever's in the freezer, and then I'll just sit on the edge of the bed. And I'll sit there for... I don't even know how long. Just staring. And then when I finally do lay down, I'm just... there. I don't really pay attention to how long it takes me to actually fall asleep. I guess I eventually do, because I wake up the next morning when my alarm goes off at 6:00 AM, but I never feel rested. Never. \n\nIt’s like I could sleep for ten hours or two hours and it wouldn't make a difference. I just wake up with that same heavy, dragging feeling, like I have to peel myself off the mattress just to go put on my scrubs and do it all over again. Sometimes I wonder if I'm even getting real sleep, or if I'm just sort of... pausing for a few hours. But I haven't really noticed, like, waking up in the middle of the night in a panic or completely unable to sleep at all. It's just... I sleep, but it doesn't do anything. It doesn't fix the tired. It's just part of the routine, just another thing I have to get through before the next shift. So, I don't know if that counts as a change, or if it's just how it is now.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling like it's harder to get things done physically since this heavy feeling started?\n\nPatient: Moving slowly? Um... I don't really think so? I mean, I don't feel like I'm physically moving in slow motion or anything like that. Like, when I'm at the clinic, I still have to hustle. You know how it is in a medical office, if I don't get the patients roomed fast enough, the whole schedule backs up and everyone gets stressed out. So I'm still walking down that long hallway to the waiting room at a normal pace, calling their names, walking them back to the scale, doing the blood pressure cuff... I don't feel like my arms and legs are actually moving slower when I'm doing all that. \n\nIt just takes so much mental energy to make them move in the first place, you know? Like, I have to sit there at my station and tell myself, 'Okay, stand up, grab the clipboard, open the door.' But once I'm doing it, I'm moving normal, I guess. It's just that the effort to do it feels so huge. But nobody at work has ever pulled me aside and said, 'Hey, why are you walking so slow?' or anything like that. I still get all the rooms turned over, I still wipe down the exam tables and restock the gloves and the alcohol swabs just as fast as I used to when I first started out of high school. \n\nIt's just... inside, I feel like I'm dragging a hundred pounds around with me everywhere I go, even if on the outside I look like I'm moving at regular speed. So, no, I don't think I'm actually moving slower physically. It's just that deep tiredness I was talking about, making every single movement feel like this massive chore I have to force myself through.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your weight over the past two years?\n\nPatient: Weight changes? Um... no, I don't think so. I mean, I don't own a scale at my apartment or anything. The only time I ever really see a scale is when I'm walking patients back to room them, and obviously I'm not hopping on it myself in the middle of a shift, right? That would be weird. But, like, my scrubs still fit the exact same way they did when I bought them right after high school. I have these three sets of navy blue ones, and they all still feel the same around the waist. I haven't had to use the drawstring any tighter or looser. \n\nAnd my regular clothes, like my jeans and stuff, they all still fit fine when I actually bother to put them on on the weekends. So I don't think I've gained or lost anything noticeable. Like I was saying before with the food, I'm just sort of eating to survive at this point, so it makes sense that my weight would just stay exactly where it is. It's just... static. I'm just existing at the exact same size, doing the exact same things, feeling the exact same way. So yeah, no changes there that I can tell. It's just all exactly the same as it's been.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding social interactions or spending less time with friends and family since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I have. I really have. It's just... I can't keep up with anything outside of work anymore. Like, at all. \n\nI used to play basketball with a couple of guys from high school. We'd always go to the courts down by the middle school, usually on Thursday nights or Sunday afternoons, just to shoot around and blow off some steam. But lately, when my phone buzzes with the group chat asking who's coming, I just stare at the screen. I don't even reply half the time. I just can't bring myself to go. And it's the same thing with this other buddy of mine. We used to do these small electronics projects together, like building custom mechanical keyboards, soldering the switches, programming the boards, stuff that takes a lot of focus and fine motor skills. We’d hang out in his garage for hours on a Saturday doing that. But the last board we started has just been sitting half-finished in a shoebox for months now. I keep telling him I'll come over and finish it, but then the weekend hits and I just... I can't. I don't have the energy to socialize or pretend I'm having fun. \n\nIt's like I have zero capacity left for my friends because every single ounce of me is just trying to survive my shifts at the clinic. And honestly, even that is falling apart now. My supervisor actually pulled me into the back office recently and put me on a formal performance plan. Which really, really sucked to hear, but I guess I get it. I've just been making way too many intake mistakes. I'll write down the wrong blood pressure, or forget to ask about medication updates, or mix up the charts... just dumb, careless stuff that I *know* how to do. My brain is just so completely fried. \n\nSo it's like... if I can't even handle my actual job without messing up and getting written up, how am I supposed to have the energy to run up and down a basketball court or sit around soldering tiny wires and making small talk? I just can't keep up with any of it. I go to work, I mess up, I come home, I sit on the edge of the bed. That's pretty much my whole life right now. So yeah, I haven't really been seeing anybody.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things that have happened?\n\nPatient: Guilty? Um... I mean, I guess I feel bad about messing up at work, like I was just saying. But I don't know if I'd call it guilt, exactly. It's more just... frustration? Like, I know I'm making these stupid mistakes with the intakes and the blood pressures and everything, and I know it makes more work for the nurses and the doctors, which sucks. And I hate that my supervisor had to sit me down and do that whole performance plan thing, because it's embarrassing. I know I'm letting people down there. \n\nBut as for, like, blaming myself for things that have happened in my life or feeling this deep, overwhelming guilt about... I don't know, my past or whatever? Not really. I don't sit around thinking I'm a terrible person who deserves to be punished or anything like that. I don't look back at things and think, 'Oh, this is all my fault.' \n\nIt's more just that I'm so incredibly tired all the time, and I'm just trying to get through the day without completely falling apart. So when I drop the ball on something, whether it's not texting my friends back about basketball or messing up a chart at the clinic, I just feel exhausted by it. I don't really have the energy to beat myself up over it for hours on end, you know? It's just another thing that I failed at because I'm too drained to do it right. So, I don't really think I'm walking around carrying a bunch of guilt or blaming myself for things. I'm just... existing. And failing at existing, I guess. But I don't think that's the same as guilt.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of two months or more where you didn't feel this heavy, low feeling?\n\nPatient: Two months where I felt okay? Um... no. No, definitely not. Honestly, there hasn't been any kind of break from it at all. \n\nIf I really sit and think about the timeline, this has been going on for about two years and two months now, completely uninterrupted. I know exactly when it started, too, because it was right around the time our clinic had this massive staffing overhaul. We lost, like, three medical assistants and a couple of front desk people all in the exact same month—they all left for this new urgent care that opened up across town because it paid a little bit more, I guess. And because they all left at once, management panicked, and my schedule just started rotating all over the place to cover the gaps. \n\nBefore that, I had a super predictable routine, just Monday through Friday, 8 to 4:30. It was fine. But suddenly, they had me doing a Tuesday through Saturday shift one week, and then four ten-hour shifts the next, and then sometimes I'd be closing the clinic at 8 PM on a Wednesday and having to be right back there to open the doors at 7 AM on Thursday. I never knew what day it was or when I was supposed to be sleeping. It just completely threw my entire life out of whack. \n\nAnd ever since that started, about two years and two months ago, this heavy feeling just settled over me and it hasn't lifted once. Not for two months, not for two weeks, probably not even for two days. It's just been this constant, unending slog since that whole scheduling mess began. Even after they finally hired new people months later and my hours leveled out a bit, the feeling just... stayed. It's like my brain just got stuck in that exhausting loop back then and never found its way out. So, yeah, no breaks at all. It's just been this one long, continuous blur of feeling weighed down.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy but no longer find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: I mean, I think I already kind of touched on that, right? With the basketball and the electronics stuff? \n\nIt's not really that I don't find pleasure in them anymore, exactly. It's more just... I don't do them. Like, I used to really love the feeling of finally getting a tricky solder joint right on those keyboards. You know, when the tin just flows perfectly around the pin and it looks like a tiny little silver Hershey's Kiss? It's really satisfying. And I used to love that feeling of sinking a three-pointer when the guys were playing pickup down at the middle school. I wasn't the best player out there, but I had a pretty decent shot from the corner. \n\nBut now... I don't know if I'd enjoy those things if I actually did them, because I just *don't* do them. I can't even get myself to the court or to my buddy's garage to find out if it would still be fun. The thought of getting dressed, driving over there, putting on a smile, and trying to focus on tiny wires or running up and down the asphalt... it just feels like trying to climb a mountain while wearing a backpack full of rocks. \n\nSo it's not like I go play basketball and think, \"Wow, I hate this now.\" It's more that I just sit on the edge of my bed and think about how exhausted I am, and the idea of doing *anything* else—even something I used to like—just feels impossible. I guess you could say I don't get pleasure from them anymore, but only because I don't have the energy to even try them in the first place. Everything just takes too much effort now.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Time of day? Um... no, not really. I mean, I've tried to track it, you know? Like, I thought maybe it was worse in the mornings because waking up is just... it's like dragging myself out of wet cement. Every single morning, the alarm goes off and I just lay there staring at the ceiling, thinking about how I have to get up and put on my scrubs and drive to the clinic and deal with all those patients and the charts and the blood pressure cuffs, and it just feels so overwhelming right from the second I open my eyes. So for a while, I was like, "Okay, mornings are the worst." 

But then I thought about it more, and honestly, the afternoons are terrible too. Like, around 2:00 or 3:00 PM, when the clinic is packed and we're running behind schedule and I'm just trying to keep my head above water, I feel this massive crash. It's not even a sleepy crash, it's just this heavy, sinking feeling where I feel like I'm literally being pulled down into the floor. And then by the time I get home at night, I'm just a zombie. I sit on the couch and stare at the wall, and the feeling is still there, just sitting on my chest like an anvil. 

So, I don't know. I can't really pinpoint a specific time. It's not like it spikes at noon and then goes away by dinner. It's just... always there. From the minute I wake up to the minute I finally manage to fall asleep, it's just this constant, heavy blanket over everything. I wish I could say, "Oh, 4:00 PM is the worst," because then maybe I could plan around it or something, but it just doesn't work like that for me. It's just a steady, flat line of feeling awful all day long.

I just can't keep up with any of it. I go to work, I mess up, I come home, I sit on the edge of the bed. That's pretty much my whole life right now. Even after they finally hired new people months later and my hours leveled out a bit, the feeling just... stayed. It's just been this one long, continuous blur of feeling weighed down.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy but no longer find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: I mean, I think I already kind of touched on that, right? Like, I used to really love the feeling of finally getting a tricky solder joint right on those keyboards. I wasn't the best player out there, but I had a pretty decent shot from the corner. \n\nBut now... I don't know if I'd enjoy those things if I actually did them, because I just *don't* do them. It's just a really deep, persistent kind of tired that I can't seem to shake, no matter what I try.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite over the past two years?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, it's funny you ask that, because... I don't really know if I'd call it a change, exactly? I mean, I guess I haven't really been paying that much attention to it, with everything else going on. When the schedule was so crazy at the clinic, I was basically just grabbing whatever I could whenever I had a second. Like, I'd eat half a protein bar while I was running between room three and room five, or I'd just chug some coffee and hope it held me over until I got home at like 8:00 PM. \n\nAnd then, even when things settled down, it just became this routine of... eating because I know I'm supposed to, I guess? I don't really think about food that much anymore. It's not like I'm starving myself or eating everything in sight. It's just... another thing I have to do to get through the day. Sometimes I'll get home and realize I haven't really eaten a real meal, so I'll just microwave something quick, but it's not because I'm craving anything. I don't really get excited about food like I used to. Like, I used to love going out and getting a burger or something on my day off, but now it just seems like too much effort to even decide what I want, let alone go get it. So I just eat whatever's easiest, just to keep going. But I wouldn't say my appetite has totally tanked or spiked or anything like that. It's just sort of... there. Like everything else, I guess. Just another part of the gray blur.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep over the past two years?\n\nPatient: Sleep? Um... honestly, I don't really know if I'd say it's changed, exactly. It’s hard to tell anymore. I mean, like I said, when I get home from the clinic, I'm just so unbelievably exhausted. My whole body just aches, and my brain feels like it's full of cotton, so you'd think I'd just pass out the second my head hits the pillow, right? But it's not really like that. \n\nI'll do my whole routine, you know, take a shower to try and wash the clinic smell off me, maybe microwave whatever's in the freezer, and then I'll just sit on the edge of the bed. And I'll sit there for... I don't even know how long. Just staring. And then when I finally do lay down, I'm just... there. I don't really pay attention to how long it takes me to actually fall asleep. I guess I eventually do, because I wake up the next morning when my alarm goes off at 6:00 AM, but I never feel rested. Never. \n\nIt’s like I could sleep for ten hours or two hours and it wouldn't make a difference. I just wake up with that same heavy, dragging feeling, like I have to peel myself off the mattress just to go put on my scrubs and do it all over again. Sometimes I wonder if I'm even getting real sleep, or if I'm just sort of... pausing for a few hours. But I haven't really noticed, like, waking up in the middle of the night in a panic or completely unable to sleep at all. It's just... I sleep, but it doesn't do anything. It doesn't fix the tired. It's just part of the routine, just another thing I have to get through before the next shift. So, I don't know if that counts as a change, or if it's just how it is now.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling like it's harder to get things done physically since this heavy feeling started?\n\nPatient: Moving slowly? Um... I don't really think so? I mean, I don't feel like I'm physically moving in slow motion or anything like that. Like, when I'm at the clinic, I still have to hustle. You know how it is in a medical office, if I don't get the patients roomed fast enough, the whole schedule backs up and everyone gets stressed out. So I'm still walking down that long hallway to the waiting room at a normal pace, calling their names, walking them back to the scale, doing the blood pressure cuff... I don't feel like my arms and legs are actually moving slower when I'm doing all that. \n\nIt just takes so much mental energy to make them move in the first place, you know? Like, I have to sit there at my station and tell myself, 'Okay, stand up, grab the clipboard, open the door.' But once I'm doing it, I'm moving normal, I guess. It's just that the effort to do it feels so huge. But nobody at work has ever pulled me aside and said, 'Hey, why are you walking so slow?' or anything like that. I still get all the rooms turned over, I still wipe down the exam tables and restock the gloves and the alcohol swabs just as fast as I used to when I first started out of high school. \n\nIt's just... inside, I feel like I'm dragging a hundred pounds around with me everywhere I go, even if on the outside I look like I'm moving at regular speed. So, no, I don't think I'm actually moving slower physically. It's just that deep tiredness I was talking about, making every single movement feel like this massive chore I have to force myself through.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your weight over the past two years?\n\nPatient: Weight changes? Um... no, I don't think so. I mean, I don't own a scale at my apartment or anything. The only time I ever really see a scale is when I'm walking patients back to room them, and obviously I'm not hopping on it myself in the middle of a shift, right? That would be weird. But, like, my scrubs still fit the exact same way they did when I bought them right after high school. I have these three sets of navy blue ones, and they all still feel the same around the waist. I haven't had to use the drawstring any tighter or looser. \n\nAnd my regular clothes, like my jeans and stuff, they all still fit fine when I actually bother to put them on on the weekends. So I don't think I've gained or lost anything noticeable. Like I was saying before with the food, I'm just sort of eating to survive at this point, so it makes sense that my weight would just stay exactly where it is. It's just... static. I'm just existing at the exact same size, doing the exact same things, feeling the exact same way. So yeah, no changes there that I can tell. It's just all exactly the same as it's been.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding social interactions or spending less time with friends and family since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I have. I really have. It's just... I can't keep up with anything outside of work anymore. Like, at all. \n\nI used to play basketball with a couple of guys from high school. We'd always go to the courts down by the middle school, usually on Thursday nights or Sunday afternoons, just to shoot around and blow off some steam. But lately, when my phone buzzes with the group chat asking who's coming, I just stare at the screen. I don't even reply half the time. I just can't bring myself to go. And it's the same thing with this other buddy of mine. We used to do these small electronics projects together, like building custom mechanical keyboards, soldering the switches, programming the boards, stuff that takes a lot of focus and fine motor skills. We’d hang out in his garage for hours on a Saturday doing that. But the last board we started has just been sitting half-finished in a shoebox for months now. I keep telling him I'll come over and finish it, but then the weekend hits and I just... I can't. I don't have the energy to socialize or pretend I'm having fun. \n\nIt's like I have zero capacity left for my friends because every single ounce of me is just trying to survive my shifts at the clinic. And honestly, even that is falling apart now. My supervisor actually pulled me into the back office recently and put me on a formal performance plan. Which really, really sucked to hear, but I guess I get it. I've just been making way too many intake mistakes. I'll write down the wrong blood pressure, or forget to ask about medication updates, or mix up the charts... just dumb, careless stuff that I *know* how to do. My brain is just so completely fried. \n\nSo it's like... if I can't even handle my actual job without messing up and getting written up, how am I supposed to have the energy to run up and down a basketball court or sit around soldering tiny wires and making small talk? I just can't keep up with any of it. I go to work, I mess up, I come home, I sit on the edge of the bed. That's pretty much my whole life right now. So yeah, I haven't really been seeing anybody.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things that have happened?\n\nPatient: Guilty? Um... I mean, I guess I feel bad about messing up at work, like I was just saying. But I don't know if I'd call it guilt, exactly. It's more just... frustration? Like, I know I'm making these stupid mistakes with the intakes and the blood pressures and everything, and I know it makes more work for the nurses and the doctors, which sucks. And I hate that my supervisor had to sit me down and do that whole performance plan thing, because it's embarrassing. I know I'm letting people down there. \n\nBut as for, like, blaming myself for things that have happened in my life or feeling this deep, overwhelming guilt about... I don't know, my past or whatever? Not really. I don't sit around thinking I'm a terrible person who deserves to be punished or anything like that. I don't look back at things and think, 'Oh, this is all my fault.' \n\nIt's more just that I'm so incredibly tired all the time, and I'm just trying to get through the day without completely falling apart. So when I drop the ball on something, whether it's not texting my friends back about basketball or messing up a chart at the clinic, I just feel exhausted by it. I don't really have the energy to beat myself up over it for hours on end, you know? It's just another thing that I failed at because I'm too drained to do it right. So, I don't really think I'm walking around carrying a bunch of guilt or blaming myself for things. I'm just... existing. And failing at existing, I guess. But I don't think that's the same as guilt.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of two months or more where you didn't feel this heavy, low feeling?\n\nPatient: Two months where I felt okay? Um... no. No, definitely not. Honestly, there hasn't been any kind of break from it at all. \n\nIf I really sit and think about the timeline, this has been going on for about two years and two months now, completely uninterrupted. I know exactly when it started, too, because it was right around the time our clinic had this massive staffing overhaul. We lost, like, three medical assistants and a couple of front desk people all in the exact same month—they all left for this new urgent care that opened up across town because it paid a little bit more, I guess. And because they all left at once, management panicked, and my schedule just started rotating all over the place to cover the gaps. \n\nBefore that, I had a super predictable routine, just Monday through Friday, 8 to 4:30. It was fine. But suddenly, they had me doing a Tuesday through Saturday shift one week, and then four ten-hour shifts the next, and then sometimes I'd be closing the clinic at 8 PM on a Wednesday and having to be right back there to open the doors at 7 AM on Thursday. I never knew what day it was or when I was supposed to be sleeping. It just completely threw my entire life out of whack. \n\nAnd ever since that started, about two years and two months ago, this heavy feeling just settled over me and it hasn't lifted once. Not for two months, not for two weeks, probably not even for two days. It's just been this constant, unending slog since that whole scheduling mess began. Even after they finally hired new people months later and my hours leveled out a bit, the feeling just... stayed. It's like my brain just got stuck in that exhausting loop back then and never found its way out. So, yeah, no breaks at all. It's just been this one long, continuous blur of feeling weighed down.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy but no longer find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: I mean, I think I already kind of touched on that, right? With the basketball and the electronics stuff? \n\nIt's not really that I don't find pleasure in them anymore, exactly. It's more just... I don't do them. Like, I used to really love the feeling of finally getting a tricky solder joint right on those keyboards. You know, when the tin just flows perfectly around the pin and it looks like a tiny little silver Hershey's Kiss? It's really satisfying. And I used to love that feeling of sinking a three-pointer when the guys were playing pickup down at the middle school. I wasn't the best player out there, but I had a pretty decent shot from the corner. \n\nBut now... I don't know if I'd enjoy those things if I actually did them, because I just *don't* do them. I can't even get myself to the court or to my buddy's garage to find out if it would still be fun. The thought of getting dressed, driving over there, putting on a smile, and trying to focus on tiny wires or running up and down the asphalt... it just feels like trying to climb a mountain while wearing a backpack full of rocks. \n\nSo it's not like I go play basketball and think, \"Wow, I hate this now.\" It's more that I just sit on the edge of my bed and think about how exhausted I am, and the idea of doing *anything* else—even something I used to like—just feels impossible. I guess you could say I don't get pleasure from them anymore, but only because I don't have the energy to even try them in the first place. Everything just takes too much effort now.\n\nDoctor: Is there a particular time of day when this heavy, low feeling is at its worst?\n\nPatient: Time of day? Um... no, not really. I mean, I've tried to track it, you know? Like, I thought maybe it was worse in the mornings because waking up is just... it's like dragging myself out of wet cement. Every single morning, the alarm goes off and I just lay there staring at the ceiling, thinking about how I have to get up and put on my scrubs and drive to the clinic and deal with all those patients and the charts and the blood pressure cuffs, and it just feels so overwhelming right from the second I open my eyes. So for a while, I was like, \"Okay, mornings are the worst.\" \n\nBut then I thought about it more, and honestly, the afternoons are terrible too. Like, around 2:00 or 3:00 PM, when the clinic is packed and we're running behind schedule and I'm just trying to keep my head above water, I feel this massive crash. It's not even a sleepy crash, it's just this heavy, sinking feeling where I feel like I'm literally being pulled down into the floor. And then by the time I get home at night, I'm just a zombie. I sit on the couch and stare at the wall, and the feeling is still there, just sitting on my chest like an anvil. \n\nSo, I don't know. I can't really pinpoint a specific time. It's not like it spikes at noon and then goes away by dinner. It's just... always there. From the minute I wake up to the minute I finally manage to fall asleep, it's just this constant, heavy blanket over everything. I wish I could say, \"Oh, 4:00 PM is the worst,\" because then maybe I could plan around it or something, but it just doesn't work like that for me. It's just a steady, flat line of feeling awful all day long.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I mean, I kind of just said that, didn't I? About it being a steady, flat line all day? 

Like, I really wish I could say there were fluctuations. I really do. I wish I could just wake up one morning and not feel that immediate weight on my chest before I even swing my legs out of bed. \n\nSometimes, when I'm rooming my fifth patient of the morning and they're complaining about something minor, I just catch myself staring at the computer screen, charting their vitals, and I just feel so disconnected, but it never crosses over into wanting to end things. It's just... I want the grayness to stop. I want to feel like a normal twenty-year-old who can actually figure out what to do on a day off instead of just staring at the wall until it's time to sleep again. So, no, I don't have those kinds of thoughts. It's just a really deep, persistent kind of tired that I can't seem to shake, no matter what I try.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite over the past two years?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, it's funny you ask that, because... I don't really know if I'd call it a change, exactly? I mean, I guess I haven't really been paying that much attention to it, with everything else going on. When the schedule was so crazy at the clinic, I was basically just grabbing whatever I could whenever I had a second. Like, I'd eat half a protein bar while I was running between room three and room five, or I'd just chug some coffee and hope it held me over until I got home at like 8:00 PM. \n\nAnd then, even when things settled down, it just became this routine of... eating because I know I'm supposed to, I guess? I don't really think about food that much anymore. It's not like I'm starving myself or eating everything in sight. It's just... another thing I have to do to get through the day. Sometimes I'll get home and realize I haven't really eaten a real meal, so I'll just microwave something quick, but it's not because I'm craving anything. I don't really get excited about food like I used to. Like, I used to love going out and getting a burger or something on my day off, but now it just seems like too much effort to even decide what I want, let alone go get it. So I just eat whatever's easiest, just to keep going. But I wouldn't say my appetite has totally tanked or spiked or anything like that. It's just sort of... there. Like everything else, I guess. Just another part of the gray blur.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep over the past two years?\n\nPatient: Sleep? Um... honestly, I don't really know if I'd say it's changed, exactly. It’s hard to tell anymore. I mean, like I said, when I get home from the clinic, I'm just so unbelievably exhausted. My whole body just aches, and my brain feels like it's full of cotton, so you'd think I'd just pass out the second my head hits the pillow, right? But it's not really like that. \n\nI'll do my whole routine, you know, take a shower to try and wash the clinic smell off me, maybe microwave whatever's in the freezer, and then I'll just sit on the edge of the bed. And I'll sit there for... I don't even know how long. Just staring. And then when I finally do lay down, I'm just... there. I don't really pay attention to how long it takes me to actually fall asleep. I guess I eventually do, because I wake up the next morning when my alarm goes off at 6:00 AM, but I never feel rested. Never. \n\nIt’s like I could sleep for ten hours or two hours and it wouldn't make a difference. I just wake up with that same heavy, dragging feeling, like I have to peel myself off the mattress just to go put on my scrubs and do it all over again. Sometimes I wonder if I'm even getting real sleep, or if I'm just sort of... pausing for a few hours. But I haven't really noticed, like, waking up in the middle of the night in a panic or completely unable to sleep at all. It's just... I sleep, but it doesn't do anything. It doesn't fix the tired. It's just part of the routine, just another thing I have to get through before the next shift. So, I don't know if that counts as a change, or if it's just how it is now.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling like it's harder to get things done physically since this heavy feeling started?\n\nPatient: Moving slowly? Um... I don't really think so? I mean, I don't feel like I'm physically moving in slow motion or anything like that. Like, when I'm at the clinic, I still have to hustle. You know how it is in a medical office, if I don't get the patients roomed fast enough, the whole schedule backs up and everyone gets stressed out. So I'm still walking down that long hallway to the waiting room at a normal pace, calling their names, walking them back to the scale, doing the blood pressure cuff... I don't feel like my arms and legs are actually moving slower when I'm doing all that. \n\nIt just takes so much mental energy to make them move in the first place, you know? Like, I have to sit there at my station and tell myself, 'Okay, stand up, grab the clipboard, open the door.' But once I'm doing it, I'm moving normal, I guess. It's just that the effort to do it feels so huge. But nobody at work has ever pulled me aside and said, 'Hey, why are you walking so slow?' or anything like that. I still get all the rooms turned over, I still wipe down the exam tables and restock the gloves and the alcohol swabs just as fast as I used to when I first started out of high school. \n\nIt's just... inside, I feel like I'm dragging a hundred pounds around with me everywhere I go, even if on the outside I look like I'm moving at regular speed. So, no, I don't think I'm actually moving slower physically. It's just that deep tiredness I was talking about, making every single movement feel like this massive chore I have to force myself through.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your weight over the past two years?\n\nPatient: Weight changes? Um... no, I don't think so. I mean, I don't own a scale at my apartment or anything. The only time I ever really see a scale is when I'm walking patients back to room them, and obviously I'm not hopping on it myself in the middle of a shift, right? That would be weird. But, like, my scrubs still fit the exact same way they did when I bought them right after high school. I have these three sets of navy blue ones, and they all still feel the same around the waist. I haven't had to use the drawstring any tighter or looser. \n\nAnd my regular clothes, like my jeans and stuff, they all still fit fine when I actually bother to put them on on the weekends. So I don't think I've gained or lost anything noticeable. Like I was saying before with the food, I'm just sort of eating to survive at this point, so it makes sense that my weight would just stay exactly where it is. It's just... static. I'm just existing at the exact same size, doing the exact same things, feeling the exact same way. So yeah, no changes there that I can tell. It's just all exactly the same as it's been.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding social interactions or spending less time with friends and family since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I have. I really have. It's just... I can't keep up with anything outside of work anymore. Like, at all. \n\nI used to play basketball with a couple of guys from high school. We'd always go to the courts down by the middle school, usually on Thursday nights or Sunday afternoons, just to shoot around and blow off some steam. But lately, when my phone buzzes with the group chat asking who's coming, I just stare at the screen. I don't even reply half the time. I just can't bring myself to go. And it's the same thing with this other buddy of mine. We used to do these small electronics projects together, like building custom mechanical keyboards, soldering the switches, programming the boards, stuff that takes a lot of focus and fine motor skills. We’d hang out in his garage for hours on a Saturday doing that. But the last board we started has just been sitting half-finished in a shoebox for months now. I keep telling him I'll come over and finish it, but then the weekend hits and I just... I can't. I don't have the energy to socialize or pretend I'm having fun. \n\nIt's like I have zero capacity left for my friends because every single ounce of me is just trying to survive my shifts at the clinic. And honestly, even that is falling apart now. My supervisor actually pulled me into the back office recently and put me on a formal performance plan. Which really, really sucked to hear, but I guess I get it. I've just been making way too many intake mistakes. I'll write down the wrong blood pressure, or forget to ask about medication updates, or mix up the charts... just dumb, careless stuff that I *know* how to do. My brain is just so completely fried. \n\nSo it's like... if I can't even handle my actual job without messing up and getting written up, how am I supposed to have the energy to run up and down a basketball court or sit around soldering tiny wires and making small talk? I just can't keep up with any of it. I go to work, I mess up, I come home, I sit on the edge of the bed. That's pretty much my whole life right now. So yeah, I haven't really been seeing anybody.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things that have happened?\n\nPatient: Guilty? Um... I mean, I guess I feel bad about messing up at work, like I was just saying. But I don't know if I'd call it guilt, exactly. It's more just... frustration? Like, I know I'm making these stupid mistakes with the intakes and the blood pressures and everything, and I know it makes more work for the nurses and the doctors, which sucks. And I hate that my supervisor had to sit me down and do that whole performance plan thing, because it's embarrassing. I know I'm letting people down there. \n\nBut as for, like, blaming myself for things that have happened in my life or feeling this deep, overwhelming guilt about... I don't know, my past or whatever? Not really. I don't sit around thinking I'm a terrible person who deserves to be punished or anything like that. I don't look back at things and think, 'Oh, this is all my fault.' \n\nIt's more just that I'm so incredibly tired all the time, and I'm just trying to get through the day without completely falling apart. So when I drop the ball on something, whether it's not texting my friends back about basketball or messing up a chart at the clinic, I just feel exhausted by it. I don't really have the energy to beat myself up over it for hours on end, you know? It's just another thing that I failed at because I'm too drained to do it right. So, I don't really think I'm walking around carrying a bunch of guilt or blaming myself for things. I'm just... existing. And failing at existing, I guess. But I don't think that's the same as guilt.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of two months or more where you didn't feel this heavy, low feeling?\n\nPatient: Two months where I felt okay? Um... no. No, definitely not. Honestly, there hasn't been any kind of break from it at all. \n\nIf I really sit and think about the timeline, this has been going on for about two years and two months now, completely uninterrupted. I know exactly when it started, too, because it was right around the time our clinic had this massive staffing overhaul. We lost, like, three medical assistants and a couple of front desk people all in the exact same month—they all left for this new urgent care that opened up across town because it paid a little bit more, I guess. And because they all left at once, management panicked, and my schedule just started rotating all over the place to cover the gaps. \n\nBefore that, I had a super predictable routine, just Monday through Friday, 8 to 4:30. It was fine. But suddenly, they had me doing a Tuesday through Saturday shift one week, and then four ten-hour shifts the next, and then sometimes I'd be closing the clinic at 8 PM on a Wednesday and having to be right back there to open the doors at 7 AM on Thursday. I never knew what day it was or when I was supposed to be sleeping. It just completely threw my entire life out of whack. \n\nAnd ever since that started, about two years and two months ago, this heavy feeling just settled over me and it hasn't lifted once. Not for two months, not for two weeks, probably not even for two days. It's just been this constant, unending slog since that whole scheduling mess began. Even after they finally hired new people months later and my hours leveled out a bit, the feeling just... stayed. It's like my brain just got stuck in that exhausting loop back then and never found its way out. So, yeah, no breaks at all. It's just been this one long, continuous blur of feeling weighed down.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy but no longer find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: I mean, I think I already kind of touched on that, right? With the basketball and the electronics stuff? \n\nIt's not really that I don't find pleasure in them anymore, exactly. It's more just... I don't do them. Like, I used to really love the feeling of finally getting a tricky solder joint right on those keyboards. You know, when the tin just flows perfectly around the pin and it looks like a tiny little silver Hershey's Kiss? It's really satisfying. And I used to love that feeling of sinking a three-pointer when the guys were playing pickup down at the middle school. I wasn't the best player out there, but I had a pretty decent shot from the corner. \n\nBut now... I don't know if I'd enjoy those things if I actually did them, because I just *don't* do them. I can't even get myself to the court or to my buddy's garage to find out if it would still be fun. The thought of getting dressed, driving over there, putting on a smile, and trying to focus on tiny wires or running up and down the asphalt... it just feels like trying to climb a mountain while wearing a backpack full of rocks. \n\nSo it's not like I go play basketball and think, \"Wow, I hate this now.\" It's more that I just sit on the edge of my bed and think about how exhausted I am, and the idea of doing *anything* else—even something I used to like—just feels impossible. I guess you could say I don't get pleasure from them anymore, but only because I don't have the energy to even try them in the first place. Everything just takes too much effort now.\n\nDoctor: Is there a particular time of day when this heavy, low feeling is at its worst?\n\nPatient: Time of day? Um... no, not really. I mean, I've tried to track it, you know? Like, I thought maybe it was worse in the mornings because waking up is just... it's like dragging myself out of wet cement. Every single morning, the alarm goes off and I just lay there staring at the ceiling, thinking about how I have to get up and put on my scrubs and drive to the clinic and deal with all those patients and the charts and the blood pressure cuffs, and it just feels so overwhelming right from the second I open my eyes. So for a while, I was like, \"Okay, mornings are the worst.\" \n\nBut then I thought about it more, and honestly, the afternoons are terrible too. Like, around 2:00 or 3:00 PM, when the clinic is packed and we're running behind schedule and I'm just trying to keep my head above water, I feel this massive crash. It's not even a sleepy crash, it's just this heavy, sinking feeling where I feel like I'm literally being pulled down into the floor. And then by the time I get home at night, I'm just a zombie. I sit on the couch and stare at the wall, and the feeling is still there, just sitting on my chest like an anvil. \n\nSo, I don't know. I can't really pinpoint a specific time. It's not like it spikes at noon and then goes away by dinner. It's just... always there. From the minute I wake up to the minute I finally manage to fall asleep, it's just this constant, heavy blanket over everything. I wish I could say, \"Oh, 4:00 PM is the worst,\" because then maybe I could plan around it or something, but it just doesn't work like that for me. It's just a steady, flat line of feeling awful all day long.\n\nDoctor: Is your low feeling steady throughout the day or are there times when it feels slightly better or worse?\n\nPatient: I mean, I kind of just said that, didn't I? About it being a steady, flat line all day? \n\nLike, I really wish I could say there were fluctuations. I really do. Because if there were times when it felt even a tiny bit better, like maybe just a five percent improvement after I eat lunch or something, then I could cling to that. I could say, \"Okay, just make it to noon and you'll get a little breather.\" But it's not like that. It's just this constant, unrelenting weight. \n\nAnd it's not like it gets drastically worse at certain times either, like a sudden panic attack or anything. It's just... it's just always exactly the same level of heavy. Like I said, from the moment I wake up and have to drag myself out of bed, through the entire shift at the clinic, dealing with all the patients and the charts, all the way until I'm just sitting on my couch at night staring at the wall... it never wavers. It doesn't get better, it doesn't get worse. It's just a continuous, flat line of this low, awful feeling. So yeah, no, there aren't times when it feels slightly better or worse. It's just always there, exactly the same, all day long.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Time of year? Um... no, I haven't really noticed anything like that. I mean, people always talk about seasonal depression, right? Like getting the winter blues when it gets dark at 4:30 in the afternoon and it's freezing cold out. And I get why that would make people miserable. I really do. When it's January and you're scraping ice off your windshield before the sun is even up, it's pretty bleak. But for me... it doesn't really seem to matter if it's January or July. 

Like, this past summer, everyone at the clinic was so excited about the weather. The nurses were talking about their weekend trips to the lake, and the doctors were talking about getting out on the golf course, and everyone just seemed so energized by the sunshine and the longer days. And I remember walking out of work one Friday in July, and the sky was this perfect, clear blue, and it was warm, and everyone was smiling and talking about their plans... and I just felt exactly the same as I do in the middle of a blizzard. It didn't touch me at all. The sun was shining, but I was still just carrying around that same heavy backpack of rocks, you know? 

And then the fall came, and the leaves changed, and everyone got excited about Halloween and Thanksgiving, and I still just felt... flat. Just that same steady, low feeling. It's like I'm completely disconnected from whatever is happening outside the window. The seasons change, the weather changes, the holidays come and go, but my mood just stays locked in this one awful place. So, no, I don't think it has anything to do with the time of year. I wish it did, honestly, because then at least I could look forward to spring or something. But for me, it's just an everyday, all-year-round kind of thing.

Before that, I had a pretty set schedule, you know? I knew when I was coming in, I knew which doctors I was working with, I knew the flow of the day. But then they started rotating my schedule all over the place. One week I’d be opening at 6:30 AM, the next week I’d be closing at 7:00 PM, and sometimes they’d even split my shifts or move me to a different department entirely. It was just constant chaos. And right around then, that's when this feeling started creeping in. At first, I thought I was just tired from the weird hours, but then the schedule settled down a little bit, and the feeling... it didn't. It just stayed. Two years and two months of waking up like this, every single day, no matter what shift I'm on or how much sleep I get. It's just always there, ever since that whole staffing mess started.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I... I honestly don't even really know how to answer that anymore. It’s like... I try to think about what I used to do for fun before all this started, before the whole clinic schedule got flipped upside down and this heavy feeling moved in, and I just draw a complete blank. I mean, I know I must have done things, right? Just normal stuff a twenty-year-old does when they aren't working. But now, when I finally clock out and get home, and my feet are just aching from running around the exam rooms all day, I just... I don't do anything. \n\nI don't even know what I *would* do. Sometimes I'll sit there on the edge of my bed and think, 'Okay, I should do something I like,' but then nothing sounds good. Nothing feels good. It's just... flat. Everything is just completely flat. It’s like I’m just trying to pass the time until it’s an acceptable hour to go to sleep, just so I don't have to feel so drained for a few hours. So, I don't really know. I haven't really noticed myself enjoying... well, anything, really. It’s just all sort of blended together into this one long, gray blur where nothing really stands out as fun or pleasurable at all. I just don't have the energy to even figure out what I might like anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had thoughts of hurting yourself or wished you were dead?\n\nPatient: No, no, I... I haven't really had thoughts like that. I mean, I know I talk a lot about how heavy everything feels, and how exhausting it is to just get through a shift at the clinic, but it's not like I want to... to hurt myself or anything. It's more just this constant, draining fatigue, you know? Like, I just want a break from feeling so low all the time. But I don't sit around wishing I was dead. I just wish things felt different. I wish I could just wake up one morning and not feel that immediate weight on my chest before I even swing my legs out of bed. \n\nSometimes, when I'm rooming my fifth patient of the morning and they're complaining about something minor, I just catch myself staring at the computer screen, charting their vitals, and I just feel so disconnected, but it never crosses over into wanting to end things. It's just... I want the grayness to stop. I want to feel like a normal twenty-year-old who can actually figure out what to do on a day off instead of just staring at the wall until it's time to sleep again. So, no, I don't have those kinds of thoughts. It's just a really deep, persistent kind of tired that I can't seem to shake, no matter what I try.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite over the past two years?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, it's funny you ask that, because... I don't really know if I'd call it a change, exactly? I mean, I guess I haven't really been paying that much attention to it, with everything else going on. When the schedule was so crazy at the clinic, I was basically just grabbing whatever I could whenever I had a second. Like, I'd eat half a protein bar while I was running between room three and room five, or I'd just chug some coffee and hope it held me over until I got home at like 8:00 PM. \n\nAnd then, even when things settled down, it just became this routine of... eating because I know I'm supposed to, I guess? I don't really think about food that much anymore. It's not like I'm starving myself or eating everything in sight. It's just... another thing I have to do to get through the day. Sometimes I'll get home and realize I haven't really eaten a real meal, so I'll just microwave something quick, but it's not because I'm craving anything. I don't really get excited about food like I used to. Like, I used to love going out and getting a burger or something on my day off, but now it just seems like too much effort to even decide what I want, let alone go get it. So I just eat whatever's easiest, just to keep going. But I wouldn't say my appetite has totally tanked or spiked or anything like that. It's just sort of... there. Like everything else, I guess. Just another part of the gray blur.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep over the past two years?\n\nPatient: Sleep? Um... honestly, I don't really know if I'd say it's changed, exactly. It’s hard to tell anymore. I mean, like I said, when I get home from the clinic, I'm just so unbelievably exhausted. My whole body just aches, and my brain feels like it's full of cotton, so you'd think I'd just pass out the second my head hits the pillow, right? But it's not really like that. \n\nI'll do my whole routine, you know, take a shower to try and wash the clinic smell off me, maybe microwave whatever's in the freezer, and then I'll just sit on the edge of the bed. And I'll sit there for... I don't even know how long. Just staring. And then when I finally do lay down, I'm just... there. I don't really pay attention to how long it takes me to actually fall asleep. I guess I eventually do, because I wake up the next morning when my alarm goes off at 6:00 AM, but I never feel rested. Never. \n\nIt’s like I could sleep for ten hours or two hours and it wouldn't make a difference. I just wake up with that same heavy, dragging feeling, like I have to peel myself off the mattress just to go put on my scrubs and do it all over again. Sometimes I wonder if I'm even getting real sleep, or if I'm just sort of... pausing for a few hours. But I haven't really noticed, like, waking up in the middle of the night in a panic or completely unable to sleep at all. It's just... I sleep, but it doesn't do anything. It doesn't fix the tired. It's just part of the routine, just another thing I have to get through before the next shift. So, I don't know if that counts as a change, or if it's just how it is now.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling like it's harder to get things done physically since this heavy feeling started?\n\nPatient: Moving slowly? Um... I don't really think so? I mean, I don't feel like I'm physically moving in slow motion or anything like that. Like, when I'm at the clinic, I still have to hustle. You know how it is in a medical office, if I don't get the patients roomed fast enough, the whole schedule backs up and everyone gets stressed out. So I'm still walking down that long hallway to the waiting room at a normal pace, calling their names, walking them back to the scale, doing the blood pressure cuff... I don't feel like my arms and legs are actually moving slower when I'm doing all that. \n\nIt just takes so much mental energy to make them move in the first place, you know? Like, I have to sit there at my station and tell myself, 'Okay, stand up, grab the clipboard, open the door.' But once I'm doing it, I'm moving normal, I guess. It's just that the effort to do it feels so huge. But nobody at work has ever pulled me aside and said, 'Hey, why are you walking so slow?' or anything like that. I still get all the rooms turned over, I still wipe down the exam tables and restock the gloves and the alcohol swabs just as fast as I used to when I first started out of high school. \n\nIt's just... inside, I feel like I'm dragging a hundred pounds around with me everywhere I go, even if on the outside I look like I'm moving at regular speed. So, no, I don't think I'm actually moving slower physically. It's just that deep tiredness I was talking about, making every single movement feel like this massive chore I have to force myself through.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your weight over the past two years?\n\nPatient: Weight changes? Um... no, I don't think so. I mean, I don't own a scale at my apartment or anything. The only time I ever really see a scale is when I'm walking patients back to room them, and obviously I'm not hopping on it myself in the middle of a shift, right? That would be weird. But, like, my scrubs still fit the exact same way they did when I bought them right after high school. I have these three sets of navy blue ones, and they all still feel the same around the waist. I haven't had to use the drawstring any tighter or looser. \n\nAnd my regular clothes, like my jeans and stuff, they all still fit fine when I actually bother to put them on on the weekends. So I don't think I've gained or lost anything noticeable. Like I was saying before with the food, I'm just sort of eating to survive at this point, so it makes sense that my weight would just stay exactly where it is. It's just... static. I'm just existing at the exact same size, doing the exact same things, feeling the exact same way. So yeah, no changes there that I can tell. It's just all exactly the same as it's been.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding social interactions or spending less time with friends and family since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I have. I really have. It's just... I can't keep up with anything outside of work anymore. Like, at all. \n\nI used to play basketball with a couple of guys from high school. We'd always go to the courts down by the middle school, usually on Thursday nights or Sunday afternoons, just to shoot around and blow off some steam. But lately, when my phone buzzes with the group chat asking who's coming, I just stare at the screen. I don't even reply half the time. I just can't bring myself to go. And it's the same thing with this other buddy of mine. We used to do these small electronics projects together, like building custom mechanical keyboards, soldering the switches, programming the boards, stuff that takes a lot of focus and fine motor skills. We’d hang out in his garage for hours on a Saturday doing that. But the last board we started has just been sitting half-finished in a shoebox for months now. I keep telling him I'll come over and finish it, but then the weekend hits and I just... I can't. I don't have the energy to socialize or pretend I'm having fun. \n\nIt's like I have zero capacity left for my friends because every single ounce of me is just trying to survive my shifts at the clinic. And honestly, even that is falling apart now. My supervisor actually pulled me into the back office recently and put me on a formal performance plan. Which really, really sucked to hear, but I guess I get it. I've just been making way too many intake mistakes. I'll write down the wrong blood pressure, or forget to ask about medication updates, or mix up the charts... just dumb, careless stuff that I *know* how to do. My brain is just so completely fried. \n\nSo it's like... if I can't even handle my actual job without messing up and getting written up, how am I supposed to have the energy to run up and down a basketball court or sit around soldering tiny wires and making small talk? I just can't keep up with any of it. I go to work, I mess up, I come home, I sit on the edge of the bed. That's pretty much my whole life right now. So yeah, I haven't really been seeing anybody.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things that have happened?\n\nPatient: Guilty? Um... I mean, I guess I feel bad about messing up at work, like I was just saying. But I don't know if I'd call it guilt, exactly. It's more just... frustration? Like, I know I'm making these stupid mistakes with the intakes and the blood pressures and everything, and I know it makes more work for the nurses and the doctors, which sucks. And I hate that my supervisor had to sit me down and do that whole performance plan thing, because it's embarrassing. I know I'm letting people down there. \n\nBut as for, like, blaming myself for things that have happened in my life or feeling this deep, overwhelming guilt about... I don't know, my past or whatever? Not really. I don't sit around thinking I'm a terrible person who deserves to be punished or anything like that. I don't look back at things and think, 'Oh, this is all my fault.' \n\nIt's more just that I'm so incredibly tired all the time, and I'm just trying to get through the day without completely falling apart. So when I drop the ball on something, whether it's not texting my friends back about basketball or messing up a chart at the clinic, I just feel exhausted by it. I don't really have the energy to beat myself up over it for hours on end, you know? It's just another thing that I failed at because I'm too drained to do it right. So, I don't really think I'm walking around carrying a bunch of guilt or blaming myself for things. I'm just... existing. And failing at existing, I guess. But I don't think that's the same as guilt.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of two months or more where you didn't feel this heavy, low feeling?\n\nPatient: Two months where I felt okay? Um... no. No, definitely not. Honestly, there hasn't been any kind of break from it at all. \n\nIf I really sit and think about the timeline, this has been going on for about two years and two months now, completely uninterrupted. I know exactly when it started, too, because it was right around the time our clinic had this massive staffing overhaul. We lost, like, three medical assistants and a couple of front desk people all in the exact same month—they all left for this new urgent care that opened up across town because it paid a little bit more, I guess. And because they all left at once, management panicked, and my schedule just started rotating all over the place to cover the gaps. \n\nBefore that, I had a super predictable routine, just Monday through Friday, 8 to 4:30. It was fine. But suddenly, they had me doing a Tuesday through Saturday shift one week, and then four ten-hour shifts the next, and then sometimes I'd be closing the clinic at 8 PM on a Wednesday and having to be right back there to open the doors at 7 AM on Thursday. I never knew what day it was or when I was supposed to be sleeping. It just completely threw my entire life out of whack. \n\nAnd ever since that started, about two years and two months ago, this heavy feeling just settled over me and it hasn't lifted once. Not for two months, not for two weeks, probably not even for two days. It's just been this constant, unending slog since that whole scheduling mess began. Even after they finally hired new people months later and my hours leveled out a bit, the feeling just... stayed. It's like my brain just got stuck in that exhausting loop back then and never found its way out. So, yeah, no breaks at all. It's just been this one long, continuous blur of feeling weighed down.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy but no longer find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: I mean, I think I already kind of touched on that, right? With the basketball and the electronics stuff? \n\nIt's not really that I don't find pleasure in them anymore, exactly. It's more just... I don't do them. Like, I used to really love the feeling of finally getting a tricky solder joint right on those keyboards. You know, when the tin just flows perfectly around the pin and it looks like a tiny little silver Hershey's Kiss? It's really satisfying. And I used to love that feeling of sinking a three-pointer when the guys were playing pickup down at the middle school. I wasn't the best player out there, but I had a pretty decent shot from the corner. \n\nBut now... I don't know if I'd enjoy those things if I actually did them, because I just *don't* do them. I can't even get myself to the court or to my buddy's garage to find out if it would still be fun. The thought of getting dressed, driving over there, putting on a smile, and trying to focus on tiny wires or running up and down the asphalt... it just feels like trying to climb a mountain while wearing a backpack full of rocks. \n\nSo it's not like I go play basketball and think, \"Wow, I hate this now.\" It's more that I just sit on the edge of my bed and think about how exhausted I am, and the idea of doing *anything* else—even something I used to like—just feels impossible. I guess you could say I don't get pleasure from them anymore, but only because I don't have the energy to even try them in the first place. Everything just takes too much effort now.\n\nDoctor: Is there a particular time of day when this heavy, low feeling is at its worst?\n\nPatient: Time of day? Um... no, not really. I mean, I've tried to track it, you know? Like, I thought maybe it was worse in the mornings because waking up is just... it's like dragging myself out of wet cement. Every single morning, the alarm goes off and I just lay there staring at the ceiling, thinking about how I have to get up and put on my scrubs and drive to the clinic and deal with all those patients and the charts and the blood pressure cuffs, and it just feels so overwhelming right from the second I open my eyes. So for a while, I was like, \"Okay, mornings are the worst.\" \n\nBut then I thought about it more, and honestly, the afternoons are terrible too. Like, around 2:00 or 3:00 PM, when the clinic is packed and we're running behind schedule and I'm just trying to keep my head above water, I feel this massive crash. It's not even a sleepy crash, it's just this heavy, sinking feeling where I feel like I'm literally being pulled down into the floor. And then by the time I get home at night, I'm just a zombie. I sit on the couch and stare at the wall, and the feeling is still there, just sitting on my chest like an anvil. \n\nSo, I don't know. I can't really pinpoint a specific time. It's not like it spikes at noon and then goes away by dinner. It's just... always there. From the minute I wake up to the minute I finally manage to fall asleep, it's just this constant, heavy blanket over everything. I wish I could say, \"Oh, 4:00 PM is the worst,\" because then maybe I could plan around it or something, but it just doesn't work like that for me. It's just a steady, flat line of feeling awful all day long.\n\nDoctor: Is your low feeling steady throughout the day or are there times when it feels slightly better or worse?\n\nPatient: I mean, I kind of just said that, didn't I? About it being a steady, flat line all day? \n\nLike, I really wish I could say there were fluctuations. I really do. Because if there were times when it felt even a tiny bit better, like maybe just a five percent improvement after I eat lunch or something, then I could cling to that. I could say, \"Okay, just make it to noon and you'll get a little breather.\" But it's not like that. It's just this constant, unrelenting weight. \n\nAnd it's not like it gets drastically worse at certain times either, like a sudden panic attack or anything. It's just... it's just always exactly the same level of heavy. Like I said, from the moment I wake up and have to drag myself out of bed, through the entire shift at the clinic, dealing with all the patients and the charts, all the way until I'm just sitting on my couch at night staring at the wall... it never wavers. It doesn't get better, it doesn't get worse. It's just a continuous, flat line of this low, awful feeling. So yeah, no, there aren't times when it feels slightly better or worse. It's just always there, exactly the same, all day long.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your low feeling is related to the time of year or seasonal changes?\n\nPatient: Time of year? Um... no, I haven't really noticed anything like that. I mean, people always talk about seasonal depression, right? Like getting the winter blues when it gets dark at 4:30 in the afternoon and it's freezing cold out. And I get why that would make people miserable. I really do. When it's January and you're scraping ice off your windshield before the sun is even up, it's pretty bleak. But for me... it doesn't really seem to matter if it's January or July. \n\nLike, this past summer, everyone at the clinic was so excited about the weather. The nurses were talking about their weekend trips to the lake, and the doctors were talking about getting out on the golf course, and everyone just seemed so energized by the sunshine and the longer days. And I remember walking out of work one Friday in July, and the sky was this perfect, clear blue, and it was warm, and everyone was smiling and talking about their plans... and I just felt exactly the same as I do in the middle of a blizzard. It didn't touch me at all. The sun was shining, but I was still just carrying around that same heavy backpack of rocks, you know? \n\nAnd then the fall came, and the leaves changed, and everyone got excited about Halloween and Thanksgiving, and I still just felt... flat. Just that same steady, low feeling. It's like I'm completely disconnected from whatever is happening outside the window. The seasons change, the weather changes, the holidays come and go, but my mood just stays locked in this one awful place. So, no, I don't think it has anything to do with the time of year. I wish it did, honestly, because then at least I could look forward to spring or something. But for me, it's just an everyday, all-year-round kind of thing.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um... no, not really. I mean, I really try to think back and find a time, like maybe a weekend or a vacation or something where I felt lighter, but... no. It's just always there. Honestly, that's kind of why I finally came in to see you. Because for a long time now, I just... I wake up already feeling low. Like, before my feet even hit the floor, before I even open my eyes all the way, there's just this heavy, gray feeling on me. 

And it's not just a morning thing, like I was saying before. It's like I put on this heavy, wet, gray coat before I even get out of bed, and I wear it into the kitchen to make coffee, and I wear it in the car on the way to work, and then I wear it all day at the clinic. It pretty much follows me through the whole day, every single day. I'll be taking a patient's vitals, or inputting data into the computer, or just standing in the breakroom trying to eat a sandwich, and I can just feel it there, weighing me down. 

I kept waiting for it to break, you know? Like, you hear about people having a bad week, or a funk, and then they snap out of it. They go out with friends or they watch a funny movie and suddenly the clouds part. But for me, the clouds just... they never part. Not for a week, not for a few days, not even for an hour. It's just this constant, endless gray. So, no, I haven't had any period where it lifted. It's just... it's just been this way for so long now, and it just follows me everywhere.

I... wait, didn't you just ask me that? Like, literally just a second ago? I'm sorry, I don't mean to be rude, I just... I thought I just answered that. I mean, you asked if there was a short period, like a few days or a week, where the heavy feeling lifted, and now you're asking if there was a short period where I felt significantly better. Because my answer is exactly the same. No. No, I haven't. I try to look back and think, okay, maybe there was a time I'm forgetting. Maybe there was a weekend last month where I went to the grocery store and actually felt okay picking out apples, or maybe there was a shift at the clinic where I actually smiled at a patient and meant it. But there isn't. There really isn't. 

It's just this constant, unrelenting slog. Every single day is exactly the same as the one before it. I wake up, the gray coat is there, I drag myself through the day, the gray coat is there, I go to bed, the gray coat is there. I don't know how else to explain it. I really don't.
